“等我到了你府上——现在想起来，我根本不认得路——我该怎么办？按门铃递名帖？还是砸碎最近的窗户爬进去？”
“千万动静别太大。仆人全睡了。你会看见我房间的窗户。就在门廊正上方。唯一亮着灯的那扇。朝它扔颗石子，我就下来。”
“玻璃也会下来——我估计还是带助跑的。不过我尽量少搞破坏。毕竟我没必要扔砖头。”
“你*一定*来，是不是？”
“什么都拦不住我。”
“好汉。”
怀亚特正要走，内维尔-史密斯忽然良心不安，把他叫住。
“我说，你不觉得太悬吗？我是说，你倒是总夜里翻出去，不是吗？我可不想害你挨整。”
“哦，没事。”怀亚特说，“别替我操心。反正今晚我横竖要出去。”

## XXIII A Surprise for Mr. Appleby / 二十三 阿普尔比先生的意外
===Original===

“You may not know it,” said Wyatt to Mike in the dormitory that night, “but this is the maddest, merriest day of all the glad New Year.”
Mike could not help thinking that for himself it was the very reverse, but he did not state his view of the case.
“What’s up?” he asked.
“Neville-Smith’s giving a meal at his place in honour of his getting his first. I understand the preparations are on a scale of the utmost magnificence. No expense has been spared. Ginger-beer will flow like water. The oldest cask of lemonade has been broached; and a sardine is roasting whole in the marketplace.”
“Are you going?”
“If I can tear myself away from your delightful society. The kickoff is fixed for eleven sharp. I am to stand underneath his window and heave bricks till something happens. I don’t know if he keeps a dog. If so, I shall probably get bitten to the bone.”
“When are you going to start?”
“About five minutes after Wain has been round the dormitories to see that all’s well. That ought to be somewhere about half-past ten.”
“Don’t go getting caught.”
“I shall do my little best not to be. Rather tricky work, though, getting back. I’ve got to climb two garden walls, and I shall probably be so full of Malvoisie that you’ll be able to hear it swishing about inside me. No catch steeple-chasing if you’re like that. They’ve no thought for people’s convenience here. Now at Bradford they’ve got studies on the ground floor, the windows looking out over the boundless prairie. No climbing or steeple-chasing needed at all. All you have to do is to open the window and step out. Still, we must make the best of things. Push us over a pinch of that tooth-powder of yours. I’ve used all mine.”
Wyatt very seldom penetrated further than his own garden on the occasions when he roamed abroad at night. For cat-shooting the Wain spinneys were unsurpassed. There was one particular dustbin where one might be certain of flushing a covey any night; and the wall by the potting-shed was a feline clubhouse.
But when he did wish to get out into the open country he had a special route which he always took. He climbed down from the wall that ran beneath the dormitory window into the garden belonging to Mr. Appleby, the master who had the house next to Mr. Wain’s. Crossing this, he climbed another wall, and dropped from it into a small lane which ended in the main road leading to Wrykyn town.

===Chinese===

“你可能不知道，”当晚怀亚特在宿舍里对迈克说，“今天可是这快活新一年里最疯、最快活的一天。”
迈克忍不住想，对他自己来说这天恰恰相反，但他没说出自己的看法。
“怎么了？”他问。
“内维尔-史密斯在他屋里开宴，庆祝他进一队。据我所知，筹备的规模奢华到了极点。不惜工本。姜汁啤酒将流得像水。最陈的一桶柠檬水已经开了；沙丁鱼正在市场上整条烤着呢。”
“你去吗？”
“要是我能从你这可爱的社交圈里挣脱出来的话。开席时间定在十一点整。我的任务是在他窗户底下扔砖头，直到出事为止。不知道他养不养狗。养的话，我多半会给啃到骨头。”
“什么时候动身？”
“大约韦恩查完各宿舍、确认天下太平之后五分钟。那大概在十点半前后。”
“可别叫人逮着。”
“我会尽我小小的努力不叫逮着。不过回来的活计相当刁钻。我得翻两道花园墙，而且到时候我肚子里灌满了马尔瓦西亚，晃一晃你都能听见里头咣当响。那副样子可没法搞障碍赛。这学校真是一点不为人的方便着想。人家布拉德福德，自习室全在底层，窗外就是一望无际的大草原。压根不用爬墙不用障碍赛。你只消开窗迈出去就行。唉，咱们也只能随遇而安。把你那牙粉递一撮过来。我自己那盒用完了。”
怀亚特夜里出门溜达，十次有九次不出自家的园子。要打猫，韦恩家的小树林是天下第一。有一只特定的垃圾桶，每夜必定能惊起一窝；花棚边那道墙，则是猫族的俱乐部。
可他真想出到旷野去的时候，有一条专用的路线，回回走它。他从宿舍窗下那道墙翻下去，落进阿普尔比先生——那位住在韦恩先生隔壁的舍监——的园子。穿过园子，再翻一道墙，落进一条小巷，巷子尽头是通往雷金镇的大路。

===Original===

This was the route which he took tonight. It was a glorious July night, and the scent of the flowers came to him with a curious distinctness as he let himself down from the dormitory window. At any other time he might have made a lengthy halt, and enjoyed the scents and small summer noises, but now he felt that it would be better not to delay. There was a full moon, and where he stood he could be seen distinctly from the windows of both houses. They were all dark, it is true, but on these occasions it was best to take no risks.
He dropped cautiously into Appleby’s garden, ran lightly across it, and was in the lane within a minute.
There he paused, dusted his trousers, which had suffered on the two walls, and strolled meditatively in the direction of the town. Half-past ten had just chimed from the school clock. He was in plenty of time.
“What a night!” he said to himself, sniffing as he walked.
---
Now it happened that he was not alone in admiring the beauty of that particular night. At ten-fifteen it had struck Mr. Appleby, looking out of his study into the moonlit school grounds, that a pipe in the open would make an excellent break in his night’s work. He had acquired a slight headache as the result of correcting a batch of examination papers, and he thought that an interval of an hour in the open air before approaching the half-dozen or so papers which still remained to be looked at might do him good. The window of his study was open, but the room had got hot and stuffy. Nothing like a little fresh air for putting him right.
For a few moments he debated the rival claims of a stroll in the cricket-field and a seat in the garden. Then he decided on the latter. The little gate in the railings opposite his house might not be open, and it was a long way round to the main entrance. So he took a deck-chair which leaned against the wall, and let himself out of the back door.
He took up his position in the shadow of a fir-tree with his back to the house. From here he could see the long garden. He was fond of his garden, and spent what few moments he could spare from work and games pottering about it. He had his views as to what the ideal garden should be, and he hoped in time to tinker his own three acres up to the desired standard. At present there remained much to be done.

===Chinese===

今晚他走的就是这条路。七月夜，美不胜收，他从宿舍窗里缒下来时，花香带着一种奇异的清晰扑上鼻端。换在平时，他多半要逗留好一阵，受用这香气和夏夜细碎的声息，可此刻他觉得还是别耽搁的好。满月当空，他站的地方，两座楼的窗户都望得清清楚楚。固然都是黑的，但这种事，还是别冒一点险的好。
他小心地落进阿普尔比的园子，轻轻一溜烟跑过去，不出一分钟已在巷子里。
他在那儿停了停，掸掉翻两道墙蹭在裤子上的土，一面若有所思地朝镇上踱去。学校的钟刚敲过十点半。时间绰绰有余。
“多好的夜！”他边走边嗅，自言自语。
---
说来也巧，欣赏这个良宵美景的还不止他一个。十点一刻，阿普尔比先生从书房望进月光下的校园，忽然想到：到露天抽一斗烟，给他这晚的功课做个小憩，妙极。批完一摞考卷，他有点轻微的头痛，他想，在攻克剩下的六七份之前，到露天坐上一个钟头，兴许有好处。书房的窗开着，可屋里已经又热又闷。没什么比一点新鲜空气更能让他回过劲来的了。
他掂量了一阵两边的去处：到板球场遛个弯，还是到园子里坐坐。然后他选了后者。他家对面栅栏上那扇小门未必开着，绕正门又太远。于是他搬起靠墙的一张帆布躺椅，出了后门。
他在一棵冷杉的影子里安顿下来，背对着房子。从这里他望得见整条长园子。他爱他的园子，工作与运动之余能挤出的片刻，都消磨在里面东摸西摸。关于理想的园子该是什么样，他自有主张，他指望来日把自己这三英亩慢慢拾掇到理想的标准。眼下，有待做的事还多。

===Original===

Why not, for instance, take away those laurels at the end of the lawn, and have a flowerbed there instead? Laurels lasted all the year round, true, whereas flowers died and left an empty brown bed in the winter, but then laurels were nothing much to look at at any time, and a garden always had a beastly appearance in winter, whatever you did to it. Much better have flowers, and get a decent show for one’s money in summer at any rate.
The problem of the bed at the end of the lawn occupied his complete attention for more than a quarter of an hour, at the end of which period he discovered that his pipe had gone out.
He was just feeling for his matches to relight it when Wyatt dropped with a slight thud into his favourite herbaceous border.
The surprise, and the agony of feeling that large boots were trampling among his treasures kept him transfixed for just the length of time necessary for Wyatt to cross the garden and climb the opposite wall. As he dropped into the lane, Mr. Appleby recovered himself sufficiently to emit a sort of strangled croak, but the sound was too slight to reach Wyatt. That reveller was walking down the Wrykyn road before Mr. Appleby had left his chair.
It is an interesting point that it was the gardener rather than the schoolmaster in Mr. Appleby that first awoke to action. It was not the idea of a boy breaking out of his house at night that occurred to him first as particularly heinous; it was the fact that the boy had broken out via his herbaceous border. In four strides he was on the scene of the outrage, examining, on hands and knees, with the aid of the moonlight, the extent of the damage done.
As far as he could see, it was not serious. By a happy accident Wyatt’s boots had gone home to right and left of precious plants but not on them. With a sigh of relief Mr. Appleby smoothed over the cavities, and rose to his feet.
At this point it began to strike him that the episode affected him as a schoolmaster also.
In that startled moment when Wyatt had suddenly crossed his line of vision, he had recognised him. The moon had shone full on his face as he left the flowerbed. There was no doubt in his mind as to the identity of the intruder.
He paused, wondering how he should act. It was not an easy question. There was nothing of the spy about Mr. Appleby. He went his way openly, liked and respected by boys and masters.

===Chinese===

比如，干吗不把草坪尽头那丛月桂挪走，改做一个花坛？月桂倒是四季常青，花却要死，一到冬天只剩一床空空的褐色土；可是月桂一年到头也没什么看头，而园子到了冬天横竖都是一副鬼样子，你怎么弄也没用。还是种花好，好歹夏天能像个样子，钱不白花。
草坪尽头那个花坛的问题，占了他全部心神一刻钟还多，结束时他发现烟斗灭了。
他正摸火柴要点，怀亚特噗通一声，轻轻落在他最心爱的多年生花境里。
那一惊，加上眼看一双大靴子践踏在他的宝贝中间的痛楚，把他钉在原地动弹不得——恰恰钉够怀亚特穿过园子、翻上对面墙的工夫。等怀亚特落进巷子，阿普尔比先生才缓过劲来，发出一声类似勒住的蛙鸣，可声音太轻，传不到怀亚特耳朵里。那位宴客已走上雷金大道了，阿普尔比先生还没离开椅子。
有意思的是，阿普尔比先生身上先醒过来的，是园丁，不是教师。他头一个想起的，不是学生夜里翻墙出去何等可恶，而是那学生竟从他的多年生花境里翻了出去。四大步他跨到暴行现场，借助月光，手脚并用地查验受灾程度。
据他查看，不重。托天之福，怀亚特那双靴子左一脚右一脚都落在空当里，没踩上宝贝。阿普尔比先生松了口气，把一个个小坑抚平，站起身来。
到这时他才开始觉得，这段插曲，对他这个教师也算一桩事。
怀亚特突然闯过他视线的惊惶一瞬里，他认出他了。他离开花床时，月光正照在他脸上。闯入者是谁，他心里没有半点疑问。
他站住，盘算该怎么办。这不是个好答的题。阿普尔比先生身上没有一丝密探气。他行事坦荡，学生和同事都敬他爱他。

===Original===

He always played the game. The difficulty here was to say exactly what the game was. Sentiment, of course, bade him forget the episode, treat it as if it had never happened. That was the simple way out of the difficulty. There was nothing unsporting about Mr. Appleby. He knew that there were times when a master might, without blame, close his eyes or look the other way. If he had met Wyatt out of bounds in the daytime, and it had been possible to convey the impression that he had not seen him, he would have done so. To be out of bounds is not a particularly deadly sin. A master must check it if it occurs too frequently, but he may use his discretion.
Breaking out at night, however, was a different thing altogether. It was on another plane. There are times when a master must waive sentiment, and remember that he is in a position of trust, and owes a duty directly to his headmaster, and indirectly, through the headmaster, to the parents. He receives a salary for doing this duty, and, if he feels that sentiment is too strong for him, he should resign in favour of someone of tougher fibre.
This was the conclusion to which Mr. Appleby came over his relighted pipe. He could not let the matter rest where it was.
In ordinary circumstances it would have been his duty to report the affair to the headmaster but in the present case he thought that a slightly different course might be pursued. He would lay the whole thing before Mr. Wain, and leave him to deal with it as he thought best. It was one of the few cases where it was possible for an assistant master to fulfil his duty to a parent directly, instead of through the agency of the headmaster.
---
Knocking out the ashes of his pipe against a tree, he folded his deck-chair and went into the house. The examination papers were spread invitingly on the table, but they would have to wait. He turned down his lamp, and walked round to Wain’s.
There was a light in one of the ground-floor windows. He tapped on the window, and the sound of a chair being pushed back told him that he had been heard. The blind shot up, and he had a view of a room littered with books and papers, in the middle of which stood Mr. Wain, like a sea-beast among rocks.
Mr. Wain recognised his visitor and opened the window. Mr. Appleby could not help feeling how like Wain it was to work on a warm summer’s night in a hermetically sealed room.

===Chinese===

他向来按规矩玩。难就难在说清这回的规矩是什么。感情当然劝他把这段插曲忘掉，只当没有过。这是最省事的出路。阿普尔比先生绝非不识大体的人。他知道，有些时候，教师尽可以不咎既往，睁一只眼闭一只眼。要是白天在界外撞见怀亚特，而又装得像是没瞧见，他一定照办。越界算不上什么死罪。太频繁了自该管一管，但他可以斟酌。
可是夜里翻墙出去，完全是另一回事。那是另一个层面上的。有时候，教师必须把感情放一放，记住自己身负信托，对校长负着直接的责任，通过校长，又对家长负着间接的责任。他领薪水就是为履行这份责任；若觉得感情压不过，就该让贤给一位心肠更硬的人。
这是阿普尔比先生重新点燃烟斗后得出的结论。这事他不能就这么算了。
按常理，他该把事情报给校长，可眼下这桩，他想，不妨走一条略微不同的路子。他把整件事端到韦恩先生面前，由韦恩酌情处置。这是少有的几种情形之一：一位普通教师可以不经过校长、直接对家长尽他的责任。
---
他把烟斗在一棵树上磕净，折好帆布椅，进了屋。考卷在桌上摊得诱人，但只好让它们等等了。他捻暗油灯，绕到韦恩家去。
一层的一扇窗里有灯。他敲敲窗，椅子向后挪动的声音告诉他里面听见了。窗帘嗖地卷起，他望进一间堆满书报的屋子，屋子当中站着韦恩先生，像礁石间的一头海兽。
韦恩先生认出了来客，打开窗。阿普尔比先生不禁暗想：大热天的夏夜，韦恩关在密不透风的屋里用功，真是一点不改他的本色——

===Original===

There was always something queer and eccentric about Wyatt’s stepfather.
“Can I have a word with you, Wain?” he said.
“Appleby! Is there anything the matter? I was startled when you tapped. Exceedingly so.”
“Sorry,” said Mr. Appleby. “Wouldn’t have disturbed you, only it’s something important. I’ll climb in through here, shall I? No need to unlock the door.” And, greatly to Mr. Wain’s surprise and rather to his disapproval, Mr. Appleby vaulted on to the windowsill, and squeezed through into the room.

===Chinese===

怀亚特这位继父，身上总有一股说不出的怪。
“能同你说句话吗，韦恩？”他说。
“阿普尔比！出什么事了吗？你一敲窗，吓了我一跳。吓得不轻。”
“抱歉，”阿普尔比先生说。“要不是要紧事，也不来搅你。我就从这儿翻进去，行吗？犯不着开锁。”说着，令韦恩先生大为诧异、也颇为不以为然地，阿普尔比先生一纵身跃上窗台，挤进了屋子。

## XXIV Caught / 二十四 被抓
===Original===

“Got some rather bad news for you, I’m afraid,” began Mr. Appleby. “I’ll smoke, if you don’t mind. About Wyatt.”
“James!”
“I was sitting in my garden a few minutes ago, having a pipe before finishing the rest of my papers, and Wyatt dropped from the wall on to my herbaceous border.”
Mr. Appleby said this with a tinge of bitterness. The thing still rankled.
“James! In your garden! Impossible. Why, it is not a quarter of an hour since I left him in his dormitory.”
“He’s not there now.”
“You astound me, Appleby. I am astonished.”
“So was I.”
“How is such a thing possible? His window is heavily barred.”
“Bars can be removed.”
“You must have been mistaken.”
“Possibly,” said Mr. Appleby, a little nettled. Gaping astonishment is always apt to be irritating. “Let’s leave it at that, then. Sorry to have disturbed you.”
“No, sit down, Appleby. Dear me, this is most extraordinary. Exceedingly so. You are certain it was James?”
“Perfectly. It’s like daylight out of doors.”
Mr. Wain drummed on the table with his fingers.
“What shall I do?”
Mr. Appleby offered no suggestion.
“I ought to report it to the headmaster. That is certainly the course I should pursue.”
“I don’t see why. It isn’t like an ordinary case. You’re the parent. You can deal with the thing directly. If you come to think of it, a headmaster’s only a sort of middleman between boys and parents. He plays substitute for the parent in his absence. I don’t see why you should drag in the master at all here.”
“There is certainly something in what you say,” said Mr. Wain on reflection.
“A good deal. Tackle the boy when he comes in, and have it out with him. Remember that it must mean expulsion if you report him to the headmaster. He would have no choice. Everybody who has ever broken out of his house here and been caught has been expelled. I should strongly advise you to deal with the thing yourself.”
“I will. Yes. You are quite right, Appleby. That is a very good idea of yours. You are not going?”
“Must. Got a pile of examination papers to look over. Good night.”
“Good night.”
Mr. Appleby made his way out of the window and through the gate into his own territory in a pensive frame of mind. He was wondering what would happen. He had taken the only possible course, and, if only Wain kept his head and did not let the matter get through officially to the headmaster, things might not be so bad for Wyatt after all.

===Chinese===

“恐怕有个相当坏的消息要带给您。”阿普尔比先生开腔，“不介意我抽口烟吧。关于詹姆斯。”
“詹姆斯！”
“几分钟前我正坐在自家花园里，抽斗烟，预备批完剩下的考卷，怀亚特就从墙上掉下来，砸在我的花境上。”
阿普尔比先生带着一丝怨气说。这事还咯在心里。
“詹姆斯！在你的花园！不可能。我离开他寝室还不到一刻钟。”
“他现在不在了。”
“你震惊了我，阿普尔比。我震惊极了。”
“我也是。”
“这种事怎么可能？他窗户上铁条森森。”
“铁条可以卸。”
“你必定弄错了。”
“也许吧。”阿普尔比先生说，有点来气。目瞪口呆的惊愕总容易惹人恼。“那就到此为止吧。抱歉打扰了。”
“别，坐，阿普尔比。天哪，这太不寻常了。不寻常之至。你确定是詹姆斯？”
“确定得很。屋外亮得跟白天一样。”
韦恩先生用手指敲着桌子。
“我该怎么办？”
阿普尔比先生不献策。
“我应当报告校长。这肯定是我该走的程序。”
“我不明白为什么。这案子不比寻常。您是家长。您可以直接处置。细想起来，校长不过是学生与家长之间的一种中间人。他在家长缺席时充当替身。我看这里根本没必要把校长拖进来。”
“你这话确有道理。”韦恩先生想了想说。
“道理多着呢。等那孩子回来就截住他，跟他摊开了谈。记住，报告校长就必然意味着开除。他别无选择。凡从这里翻出去被抓住的，个个都开除了。我力劝您自行处置。”
“我自行处置。是的。你说得很对，阿普尔比。这个主意很好。你要走了？”
“必须走。还有一堆考卷要批。晚安。”
“晚安。”
阿普尔比先生从窗户出去，穿过院门回到自家地界，一路心事重重。他不知道接下来会怎样。他已走了唯一可行的一步，而只要韦恩沉得住气，别让这事经官面捅到校长那里，怀亚特的处境终究未必太糟。

===Original===

He hoped they would not. He liked Wyatt. It would be a thousand pities, he felt, if he were to be expelled. What would Wain do? What would *he* do in a similar case? It was difficult to say. Probably talk violently for as long as he could keep it up, and then consider the episode closed. He doubted whether Wain would have the common sense to do this. Altogether it was very painful and disturbing, and he was taking a rather gloomy view of the assistant master’s lot as he sat down to finish off the rest of his examination papers. It was not all roses, the life of an assistant master at a public school. He had continually to be sinking his own individual sympathies in the claims of his duty. Mr. Appleby was the last man who would willingly have reported a boy for enjoying a midnight ramble. But he was the last man to shirk the duty of reporting him, merely because it was one decidedly not to his taste.
Mr. Wain sat on for some minutes after his companion had left, pondering over the news he had heard. Even now he clung to the idea that Appleby had made some extraordinary mistake. Gradually he began to convince himself of this. He had seen Wyatt actually in bed a quarter of an hour before﻿—not asleep, it was true, but apparently on the verge of dropping off. And the bars across the window had looked so solid.﻿ ﻿… Could Appleby have been dreaming? Something of the kind might easily have happened. He had been working hard, and the night was warm.﻿ ﻿…
Then it occurred to him that he could easily prove or disprove the truth of his colleague’s statement by going to the dormitory and seeing if Wyatt were there or not. If he had gone out, he would hardly have returned yet.
He took a candle, and walked quietly upstairs.
Arrived at his stepson’s dormitory, he turned the door-handle softly and went in. The light of the candle fell on both beds. Mike was there, asleep. He grunted, and turned over with his face to the wall as the light shone on his eyes. But the other bed was empty. Appleby had been right.
If further proof had been needed, one of the bars was missing from the window. The moon shone in through the empty space.
The housemaster sat down quietly on the vacant bed. He blew the candle out, and waited there in the semidarkness, thinking. For years he and Wyatt had lived in a state of armed neutrality, broken by various small encounters.

===Chinese===

他盼着别糟。他喜欢怀亚特。这孩子若被开除，在他看来千般可惜。韦恩会怎么做？换*他*自己在类似案子里会怎么做？难说。多半是暴风骤雨地训到训不动为止，然后视为结案。他怀疑韦恩有没有这份常识。总之这事恼人捉心，等他坐下批完余下的考卷时，他已对助理教师的命运抱了相当灰暗的看法。公学助理教师的生活，并非处处玫瑰。他得不时把个人的同情沉下去，成全职责的声索。阿普尔比先生是最不愿意为一次午夜漫游举报学生的人。但他也最不会因为一件事实在不合胃口，就逃避举报的义务。
同伴走后，韦恩先生又坐了几分钟，咂摸听来的消息。直到此刻他还抱着一线指望：阿普尔比犯了什么了不得的错。渐渐地他开始把自己说服了。一刻钟前他亲眼见过怀亚特躺在床上——没睡着，不假，可分明睡意临头。窗上的铁条看着那么结实。……莫非阿普尔比在做梦？这一类的事很容易发生。他工作辛苦，夜又暖。……
随后他想到，去寝室看一眼怀亚特在不在，同僚的话真伪立判。他要是真出去了，这会儿还回不来。
他端起蜡烛，轻手轻脚上了楼。
到了继子的寝室，他轻轻拧开门把手进去。烛光落在两张床上。迈克在，睡着。灯光照到眼睛，他哼了一声，翻身脸朝墙。另一张床却是空的。阿普尔比没说错。
若还需证据，窗上少了一根铁条。月光从那空档照进来。
舍监在空床上轻轻坐下。他吹灭蜡烛，在半暗中等着，想着。多年来他和怀亚特处于武装中立状态，只被几场小遭遇战打破。

===Original===

Lately, by silent but mutual agreement, they had kept out of each other’s way as much as possible, and it had become rare for the housemaster to have to find fault officially with his stepson. But there had never been anything even remotely approaching friendship between them. Mr. Wain was not a man who inspired affection readily, least of all in those many years younger than himself. Nor did he easily grow fond of others. Wyatt he had regarded, from the moment when the threads of their lives became entangled, as a complete nuisance.
It was not, therefore, a sorrowful, so much as an exasperated, vigil that he kept in the dormitory. There was nothing of the sorrowing father about his frame of mind. He was the housemaster about to deal with a mutineer, and nothing else.
This breaking-out, he reflected wrathfully, was the last straw. Wyatt’s presence had been a nervous inconvenience to him for years. The time had come to put an end to it. It was with a comfortable feeling of magnanimity that he resolved not to report the breach of discipline to the headmaster. Wyatt should not be expelled. But he should leave, and that immediately. He would write to the bank before he went to bed, asking them to receive his stepson at once; and the letter should go by the first post next day. The discipline of the bank would be salutary and steadying. And﻿—this was a particularly grateful reflection﻿—a fortnight annually was the limit of the holiday allowed by the management to its junior employees.
Mr. Wain had arrived at this conclusion, and was beginning to feel a little cramped, when Mike Jackson suddenly sat up.
“Hullo!” said Mike.
“Go to sleep, Jackson, immediately,” snapped the housemaster.
Mike had often heard and read of people’s hearts leaping to their mouths, but he had never before experienced that sensation of something hot and dry springing in the throat, which is what really happens to us on receipt of a bad shock. A sickening feeling that the game was up beyond all hope of salvation came to him. He lay down again without a word.
What a frightful thing to happen! How on earth had this come about? What in the world had brought Wain to the dormitory at that hour? Poor old Wyatt! If it had upset *him* (Mike) to see the housemaster in the room, what would be the effect of such a sight on Wyatt, returning from the revels at Neville-Smith’s!
And what could he do?

===Chinese===

近来，凭一种无言而彼此心照的默契，他们尽量互不照面，舍监需要正式挑继子毛病的场合也少了。但两人之间从未有过任何稍稍接近友谊的东西。韦恩先生不是个轻易激发爱戴的人，对比他年轻许多的人尤其不能。他也不轻易对人产生好感。从两人的生活之线绞到一起那一刻起，他就把怀亚特看作一个大麻烦。
因此，他在寝室里守的这一班，不是悲伤的，而是狂怒的。他的心态里没有半点哀痛的父亲。他是即将处置叛乱者的舍监，仅此而已。
这桩夜翻，他怒想着，是最后一根稻草。多年来，怀亚特的存在于他是一种神经上的不便。了结的时候到了。他怀着一种宽宏大量的舒适感，决定不把这次违纪上报校长。怀亚特不会被开除。但他得走，立刻走。他要在睡前给银行写信，请他们即刻接收他的继子；信随明晨头班邮发出。银行的纪律将有裨身心，令人安稳。而且——这一想尤其熨帖——银行管理层准许低级雇员享受的假期，一年以两星期为限。
韦恩先生得出这一结论，正觉出几分坐僵，迈克·杰克逊忽然坐了起来。
“喂！”迈克说。
“睡觉去，杰克逊，立刻。”舍监厉声道。
人心跳到嗓子眼，这种事迈克常听说也常读到，可他从前从未体验过喉咙里蹿起一团又热又干的东西——那才是我们遭了大惊吓时真正发生的事。一切都完了、毫无救赎之感，令人作呕地涌上来。他一言不发重新躺下。
出了这样的事，糟透了！这到底怎么闹的？究竟什么把韦恩在这个钟点引到寝室来？可怜的老怀亚特！看见舍监坐在屋里，*他*（迈克）都吓成这样，那从内维尔-史密斯家的狂欢归来、撞见这景象的怀亚特，又当如何！
而他能做什么？

===Original===

Nothing. There was literally no way out. His mind went back to the night when he had saved Wyatt by a brilliant coup. The most brilliant of coups could effect nothing now. Absolutely and entirely the game was up.
---
Every minute that passed seemed like an hour to Mike. Dead silence reigned in the dormitory, broken every now and then by the creak of the other bed, as the housemaster shifted his position. Twelve boomed across the field from the school clock. Mike could not help thinking what a perfect night it must be for him to be able to hear the strokes so plainly. He strained his ears for any indication of Wyatt’s approach, but could hear nothing. Then a very faint scraping noise broke the stillness, and presently the patch of moonlight on the floor was darkened.
At that moment Mr. Wain relit his candle.
The unexpected glare took Wyatt momentarily aback. Mike saw him start. Then he seemed to recover himself. In a calm and leisurely manner he climbed into the room.
“James!” said Mr. Wain. His voice sounded ominously hollow.
Wyatt dusted his knees, and rubbed his hands together. “Hullo, is that you, father!” he said pleasantly.

===Chinese===

什么也做不了。字面意义上无路可出。他的思绪回到那晚，他用一手妙计救了怀亚特。如今最妙的计也无济于事。彻头彻尾、完完全全，这局完了。
---
对迈克来说，每一分钟都像一个钟头。寝室里死寂当政，只偶尔被另一张床的吱呀打破——舍监换了坐姿。十二点的钟声从学校大钟那头嗡嗡传过场地。迈克不禁想，能听得这么真切，夜该多清朗。他竖起耳朵捕捉怀亚特靠近的任何迹象，什么也听不见。随后一声极轻的刮擦划破寂静，不多时，地板上那块月光暗了。
就在此刻，韦恩先生重新点亮蜡烛。
这突如其来的亮光叫怀亚特一时愣住。迈克看见他一惊。随即他像是定住了神，以从容不迫的姿态翻进屋来。
“詹姆斯！”韦恩先生说。他的嗓音空洞得不祥。
怀亚特掸掸膝盖，搓了搓手。“喂，是您哪，父亲！”他愉快地说。

## XXV Marching Orders / 二十五 开除令
===Original===

A silence followed. To Mike, lying in bed, holding his breath, it seemed a long silence. As a matter of fact it lasted for perhaps ten seconds. Then Mr. Wain spoke.
“You have been out, James?”
It is curious how in the more dramatic moments of life the inane remark is the first that comes to us.
“Yes, sir,” said Wyatt.
“I am astonished. Exceedingly astonished.”
“I got a bit of a start myself,” said Wyatt.
“I shall talk to you in my study. Follow me there.”
“Yes, sir.”
He left the room, and Wyatt suddenly began to chuckle.
“I say, Wyatt!” said Mike, completely thrown off his balance by the events of the night.
Wyatt continued to giggle helplessly. He flung himself down on his bed, rolling with laughter. Mike began to get alarmed.
“It’s all right,” said Wyatt at last, speaking with difficulty. “But, I say, how long had he been sitting there?”
“It seemed hours. About an hour, I suppose, really.”
“It’s the funniest thing I’ve ever struck. Me sweating to get in quietly, and all the time him camping out on my bed!”
“But look here, what’ll happen?”
Wyatt sat up.
“That reminds me. Suppose I’d better go down.”
“What’ll he do, do you think?”
“Ah, now, what!”
“But, I say, it’s awful. What’ll happen?”
“That’s for him to decide. Speaking at a venture, I should say﻿—”
“You don’t think﻿—?”
“The boot. The swift and sudden boot. I shall be sorry to part with you, but I’m afraid it’s a case of ‘Au revoir, my little Hyacinth.’ We shall meet at Philippi. This is my Moscow. Tomorrow I shall go out into the night with one long, choking sob. Years hence a white-haired bank-clerk will tap at your door when you’re a prosperous professional cricketer with your photograph in *Wisden*. That’ll be me. Well, I suppose I’d better go down. We’d better all get to bed *some* time tonight. Don’t go to sleep.”
“Not likely.”
“I’ll tell you all the latest news when I come back. Where are me slippers? Ha, ’tis well! Lead on, then, minions. I follow.”
---
In the study Mr. Wain was fumbling restlessly with his papers when Wyatt appeared.
“Sit down, James,” he said.
Wyatt sat down. One of his slippers fell off with a clatter. Mr. Wain jumped nervously.
“Only my slipper,” explained Wyatt. “It slipped.”
Mr. Wain took up a pen, and began to tap the table.
“Well, James?”
Wyatt said nothing.
“I should be glad to hear your explanation of this disgraceful matter.”
“The fact is﻿—” said Wyatt.
“Well?”

===Chinese===

一阵沉默。对躺在床上屏住呼吸的迈克来说，这沉默似乎很长。实际上它也许只有十秒钟。然后韦恩先生开口了。
“你出去过，詹姆斯？”
奇怪得很，人生最富戏剧性的时刻，我们头一个想起的偏偏是句傻话。
“是的，先生。”怀亚特说。
“我很吃惊。非常吃惊。”
“我自己也吓了一跳。”怀亚特说。
“到书房再说。跟我来。”
“是，先生。”
他出了屋，怀亚特忽然咯咯笑起来。
“我说，怀亚特！”迈克叫道，这一夜的变故把他彻底撹懵了。
怀亚特还在止不住地噦笑。他一头倒在自己床上，打着滚笑。迈克开始发慌。
“没事，”怀亚特总算开口了，笑得说话都艰难。“可是，我说，他在那儿坐了多久？”
“像有几个钟头。真算起来，大概一个钟头吧。”
“这是我碰上过的最滑稽的事。我小心翼翼地熗进来，他倒好，一直在找床上安营扎寨！”
“可是，你怎么办？会怎么样呢？”
怀亚特坐起来。
“这倒提醒我了。看样子我该下去了。”
“你说他会怎么办？”
“啊，这可难说！”
“可是，我说，糟糕透了。会怎么样呢？”
“那就由他决定了。冒昧预测一句的话，我该说——”
“你不会觉得——？”
“靴子。那只又快又突然的靴子。同你分手我会很难过，可恐怕这是一出“再会了，我的小风信子”。我们菲利率见吧。这是我的莫斯科。明天夜里，我将带着一声长长哽咽的抽嗝，走进黑暗。多年以后，一个白发苍苍的银行职员会敲响你的房门——那时你已是功成名就的职业板球手，玉照登在《威斯登》上。那就会是我。好了，看样子我该下去了。今夜咱们好歹也得睡。你可别先睡着。”
“才不会呢。”
“回来给你报告最新消息。我的拖鞋呢？哈，这就好！那么带路吧，众位小厮，我来了。”
---
书房里，韦恩先生正烦躁地翻弄着他的文件，怀亚特进来了。
“坐，詹姆斯。”他说。
怀亚特坐下。一只拖鞋哌啷一声掉在地上。韦恩先生紧张地一跳。
“只是我的拖鞋，”怀亚特解释道。“它熗了。”
韦恩先生拏起一支笔，开始敲桌子。
“怎么样，詹姆斯？”
怀亚特不说话。
“这件丢人的事，我倒想听听你的解释。”
“事情是这样——”怀亚特说。
“怎么样？”

===Original===

“I haven’t one, sir.”
“What were you doing out of your dormitory, out of the house, at that hour?”
“I went for a walk, sir.”
“And, may I inquire, are you in the habit of violating the strictest school rules by absenting yourself from the house during the night?”
“Yes, sir.”
“What?”
“Yes, sir.”
“This is an exceedingly serious matter.”
Wyatt nodded agreement with this view.
“Exceedingly.”
The pen rose and fell with the rapidity of the cylinder of a motorcar. Wyatt, watching it, became suddenly aware that the thing was hypnotising him. In a minute or two he would be asleep.
“I wish you wouldn’t do that, father. Tap like that, I mean. It’s sending me to sleep.”
“James!”
“It’s like a woodpecker.”
“Studied impertinence﻿—”
“I’m very sorry. Only it *was* sending me off.”
Mr. Wain suspended tapping operations, and resumed the thread of his discourse.
“I am sorry, exceedingly, to see this attitude in you, James. It is not fitting. It is in keeping with your behaviour throughout. Your conduct has been lax and reckless in the extreme. It is possible that you imagine that the peculiar circumstances of our relationship secure you from the penalties to which the ordinary boy﻿—”
“No, sir.”
“I need hardly say,” continued Mr. Wain, ignoring the interruption, “that I shall treat you exactly as I should treat any other member of my house whom I had detected in the same misdemeanour.”
“Of course,” said Wyatt, approvingly.
“I must ask you not to interrupt me when I am speaking to you, James. I say that your punishment will be no whit less severe than would be that of any other boy. You have repeatedly proved yourself lacking in ballast and a respect for discipline in smaller ways, but this is a far more serious matter. Exceedingly so. It is impossible for me to overlook it, even were I disposed to do so. You are aware of the penalty for such an action as yours?”
“The sack,” said Wyatt laconically.
“It is expulsion. You must leave the school. At once.”
Wyatt nodded.
“As you know, I have already secured a nomination for you in the London and Oriental Bank. I shall write tomorrow to the manager asking him to receive you at once﻿—”
“After all, they only gain an extra fortnight of me.”
“You will leave directly I receive his letter. I shall arrange with the headmaster that you are withdrawn privately﻿—”
“*Not* the sack?”
“Withdrawn privately. You will not go to school tomorrow.

===Chinese===

“我没有解释，先生。”
“那个钟点，你不在宿舍、不在宿舍楼，出去干什么了？”
“出去散了个步，先生。”
“那么我可否请问，你常有半夜熗离宿舍楼的勾当、公然违反校规中最严的那几条吗？”
“是的，先生。”
“什么？”
“是的，先生。”
“这是非常严重的事。”
怀亚特点头，表示同意这个判断。
“非常。”
那支笔起起落落，快得赛过汽车的汽缸。怀亚特看着看着，忽然发觉那东西正在催眠。再过一两分钟他就要睡着了。
“您别那么敲了吧，爸。我是说别那样敲。快把我敲睡着了。”
“詹姆斯！”
“活像啄木鸟。”
“成心顶撞——”
“很抱歉。可它确实在送我入梦。”
韦恩先生暂停敲击作业，接回话头。
“我很遗憾，非常遗憾，看到你这个态度，詹姆斯。这不合体统。也同你一贯的行径一脉相承。你的品行松懈放肆到了极点。也许你自以为，我们两人的特殊关系能保你免受普通学生所受的惩处——”
“不，先生。”
“不消说，”韦恩先生不理会打断，继续道，“我会同样处置你，一如处置舍内任何一名让我查出同样过失的学生。”
“当然，”怀亚特赞同地说。
“我同你说话的时候，请你不要打断，詹姆斯。我说了，你的惩处，不会比任何别的学生轻一丝一毫。你在小的方面已一再证明自己缺乏定盘星、目无纪律，可这一桩要严重得多。严重之极。即便我有意姑息，也不可能。你知道你这种行为该当何罚吧？”
“妙魿鱼罐头，”怀亚特懒懒地说。
“是开除。你必须离校。立刻。”
怀亚特点头。
“你知道，我已经替你在伦敦东方银行谋得一个名额。我明天就写信给经理，请他即刻收你——”
“说起来，人家不过多得了两个星期的我。”
“我一收到他的回信你就动身。我会同校长安排好，让你悄悄退学——”
“不是妙魿鱼罐头？”
“悄悄退学。明天你不用上学了。明白吗？就是这样。你还有什么话要说？”
怀亚特想了想。
“没有，我想——”
他的眼睛落在一只托着细颈酒瓶和汽水壶的托盘上。
“哦，有了，”他说。“我上床之前，就不能给您调一杯威士忌苏打吗，爸？”

===Original===

Do you understand? That is all. Have you anything to say?”
Wyatt reflected.
“No, I don’t think﻿—”
His eye fell on a tray bearing a decanter and a syphon.
“Oh, yes,” he said. “Can’t I mix you a whisky and soda, father, before I go off to bed?”
---
“Well?” said Mike.
Wyatt kicked off his slippers, and began to undress.
“What happened?”
“We chatted.”
“Has he let you off?”
“Like a gun. I shoot off almost immediately. Tomorrow I take a well-earned rest away from school, and the day after I become the gay young bank-clerk, all amongst the ink and ledgers.”
Mike was miserably silent.
“Buck up,” said Wyatt cheerfully. “It would have happened anyhow in another fortnight. So why worry?”
Mike was still silent. The reflection was doubtless philosophic, but it failed to comfort him.

===Chinese===

怀亚特踢掉拖鞋，开始脱衣裳。
“怎么样了？”
“我们聊了聊。”
“他放过你了？”
“放了一炮。我马上就要发射了。明天我休一个理所应得的假，离开学校；后天我就摇身一变，做快活的年轻银行职员，整天泡在墨水和账本里。”
迈克悲惨地沉默着。
“振作点，”怀亚特欢欢地说。“反正再过两个星期也是这么回事。操那份心干吗？”
迈克还是沉默。这话想必很有哲理，可惜安慰不了他。

## XXVI The Aftermath / 二十六 余波
===Original===

Bad news spreads quickly. By the quarter to eleven interval next day the facts concerning Wyatt and Mr. Wain were public property. Mike, as an actual spectator of the drama, was in great request as an informant. As he told the story to a group of sympathisers outside the school shop, Burgess came up, his eyes rolling in a fine frenzy.
“Anybody seen young﻿—oh, here you are. What’s all this about Jimmy Wyatt? They’re saying he’s been sacked, or some rot.”
“So he has﻿—at least, he’s got to leave.”
“What? When?”
“He’s left already. He isn’t coming to school again.”
Burgess’s first thought, as befitted a good cricket captain, was for his team.
“And the Ripton match on Saturday!”
Nobody seemed to have anything except silent sympathy at his command.
“Dash the man! Silly ass! What did he want to do it for! Poor old Jimmy, though!” he added after a pause. “What rot for him!”
“Beastly,” agreed Mike.
“All the same,” continued Burgess, with a return to the austere manner of the captain of cricket, “he might have chucked playing the goat till after the Ripton match. Look here, young Jackson, you’ll turn out for fielding with the first this afternoon. You’ll play on Saturday.”
“All right,” said Mike, without enthusiasm. The Wyatt disaster was too recent for him to feel much pleasure at playing against Ripton *vice* his friend, withdrawn.
Bob was the next to interview him. They met in the cloisters.
“Hullo, Mike!” said Bob. “I say, what’s all this about Wyatt?”
“Wain caught him getting back into the dorm last night after Neville-Smith’s, and he’s taken him away from the school.”
“What’s he going to do? Going into that bank straight away?”
“Yes. You know, that’s the part he bars most. He’d have been leaving anyhow in a fortnight, you see; only it’s awful rot for a chap like Wyatt to have to go and froust in a bank for the rest of his life.”
“He’ll find it rather a change, I expect. I suppose you won’t be seeing him before he goes?”
“I shouldn’t think so. Not unless he comes to the dorm during the night. He’s sleeping over in Wain’s part of the house, but I shouldn’t be surprised if he nipped out after Wain has gone to bed. Hope he does, anyway.”
“I should like to say goodbye. But I don’t suppose it’ll be possible.”
They separated in the direction of their respective form-rooms. Mike felt bitter and disappointed at the way the news had been received.

===Chinese===

坏消息传得快。第二天十一点差一刻的课间，怀亚特和韦恩先生的事已成公产。迈克作为这场戏的现场目击者，成了抢手的情报源。他正在学校小卖部门外对一群慰问者讲述经过，伯吉斯走了过来，两眼乱转，转得漂亮极了。
“有人见着小——哦，你在这儿。吉米·怀亚特到底怎么回事？都说他被开除了，还是什么胡话。”
“是被开除了——至少，得走了。”
“什么？什么时候？”
“已经走了。不再来上学了。”
伯吉斯的头一个念头，不愧是位好队长，是他的球队。
“可星期六还要对里普顿啊！”
众人手上似乎只有无声的同情，可以奉送。
“这混蛋！蠢驴！他干吗来这一手！不过，可怜的老吉米！”他顿了顿又补一句。“他可真倒霏！”
“糟透了。”迈克附和。
“不过话说回来，”伯吉斯又摆回板球队长的严正面孔，“他也该把这出胡闹揶到对完里普顿之后啊。听我说，小杰克逊，今天下午你随一队练守场。星期六你上场。”
“好。”迈克说，兴致不高。怀亚特之祸太新，朋友被撤、自己顶替上场对里普顿，他实在高兴不起来。
下一个来问询的是鲍勃。他们在回廊碰上。
“嘷，迈克！”鲍勃说。“我说，怀亚特到底怎么了？”
“昨晚他从内维尔-史密斯那儿回来，进宿舍时叫韦恩逮了个正着，韦恩把他带离了学校。”
“他去干什么？直接进那家银行？”
“嗯。你知道，这是他最不能忍的一层。反正再过两个星期他也要走，你说是不是；只是像怀亚特这样的人，往后一辈子得蹲在银行里焐馊，实在太糟蹋了。”
“我猜他会发现变化相当大。他走之前你见不着他了？”
“我看是。除非他夜里熗回宿舍来。他现在睡在韦恩家那边，不过韦恩一睡，他说不准就熗出来。反正我希望他熗。”
“我想同他道个别。可看样子没指望了。”
两人各自朝自己的教室分开。消息受到的这般对待，让迈克又苦又失望。

===Original===

Wyatt was his best friend, his pal; and it offended him that the school should take the tidings of his departure as they had done. Most of them who had come to him for information had expressed a sort of sympathy with the absent hero of his story, but the chief sensation seemed to be one of pleasurable excitement at the fact that something big had happened to break the monotony of school routine. They treated the thing much as they would have treated the announcement that a record score had been made in first-class cricket. The school was not so much regretful as comfortably thrilled. And Burgess had actually cursed before sympathising. Mike felt resentful towards Burgess. As a matter of fact, the cricket captain wrote a letter to Wyatt during preparation that night which would have satisfied even Mike’s sense of what was fit. But Mike had no opportunity of learning this.
There was, however, one exception to the general rule, one member of the school who did not treat the episode as if it were merely an interesting and impersonal item of sensational news. Neville-Smith heard of what had happened towards the end of the interval, and rushed off instantly in search of Mike. He was too late to catch him before he went to his form-room, so he waited for him at half-past twelve, when the bell rang for the end of morning school.
“I say, Jackson, is this true about old Wyatt?”
Mike nodded.
“What happened?”
Mike related the story for the sixteenth time. It was a melancholy pleasure to have found a listener who heard the tale in the right spirit. There was no doubt about Neville-Smith’s interest and sympathy. He was silent for a moment after Mike had finished.
“It was all my fault,” he said at length. “If it hadn’t been for me, this wouldn’t have happened. What a fool I was to ask him to my place! I might have known he would be caught.”
“Oh, I don’t know,” said Mike.
“It was absolutely my fault.”
Mike was not equal to the task of soothing Neville-Smith’s wounded conscience. He did not attempt it. They walked on without further conversation till they reached Wain’s gate, where Mike left him. Neville-Smith proceeded on his way, plunged in meditation.
The result of which meditation was that Burgess got a second shock before the day was out.

===Chinese===

怀亚特是他最好的朋友，他的哥们儿；学校这样对待他离去的消息，让迈克心里发慊。来打听的人，多数对他的故事里那位缺席的主角表示了某种同情，但主要的感觉似乎是：出了件大事，打破了学校日常的单调，令人愉快地刺激。大家那态度，同听到一级板球赛破了个得分纪录的通告差不多。学校谈不上痛惜，只是一种舒舒服服的兴奋。而伯吉斯居然先骂了一句才想起同情。迈克对伯吉斯生了怨气。其实，那位板球队长当晚上自习时给怀亚特写了一封信，那封信本可以满足迈克对得体的一切要求。可惜迈克无从知道。
不过，通例之外也有一个例外，有一个没把这件事当成一条有趣的、与己无关的耸动新闻的。内维尔-史密斯在课间快结束时听说了这件事，立刻飞奔去找迈克。没赶在他进教室前堵到，就等到十二点半，上午放课的铃响。
“我说，杰克逊，老怀亚特的事是真的？”
迈克点头。
“怎么回事？”
迈克把故事讲了第十六遍。找到一个以正确态度听这故事的人，是一种悲愁的慰藉。内维尔-史密斯的关切与同情不容置疑。听完，他沉默了一阵。
“全怪我，”他末了说。“要不是为了我，就不会出这事。我多蠢，请他上我那儿去！我该想到他会被逮住的。”
“哦，那也难说。”迈克说。
“绝对是我的错。”
安懝内维尔-史密斯受伤的良心，超出迈克的能力。他也没有尝试。两人默默走了一段，到韦恩家大门口，迈克丢下他走了。内维尔-史密斯独自前行，陷入沉思。
这沉思的结果是：天还没黑，伯吉斯又换了第二记闷棍。

===Original===

Bob, going over to the nets rather late in the afternoon, came upon the captain of cricket standing apart from his fellow men with an expression on his face that spoke of mental upheavals on a vast scale.
“What’s up?” asked Bob.
“Nothing much,” said Burgess, with a forced and grisly calm. “Only that, as far as I can see, we shall play Ripton on Saturday with a sort of second eleven. You don’t happen to have got sacked or anything, by the way, do you?”
“What’s happened now?”
“Neville-Smith. In extra on Saturday. That’s all. Only our first- and second-change bowlers out of the team for the Ripton match in one day. I suppose by tomorrow half the others’ll have gone, and we shall take the field on Saturday with a scratch side of kids from the Junior School.”
“Neville-Smith! Why, what’s he been doing?”
“Apparently he gave a sort of supper to celebrate his getting his first, and it was while coming back from that that Wyatt got collared. Well, I’m blowed if Neville-Smith doesn’t toddle off to the Old Man after school today and tell him the whole yarn! Said it was all his fault. What rot! Sort of thing that might have happened to anyone. If Wyatt hadn’t gone to him, he’d probably have gone out somewhere else.”
“And the Old Man shoved him in extra?”
“Next two Saturdays.”
“Are Ripton strong this year?” asked Bob, for lack of anything better to say.
“Very, from all accounts. They whacked the M.C.C. Jolly hot team of M.C.C. too. Stronger than the one we drew with.”
“Oh, well, you never know what’s going to happen at cricket. I may hold a catch for a change.”
Burgess grunted.
Bob went on his way to the nets. Mike was just putting on his pads.
“I say, Mike,” said Bob. “I wanted to see you. It’s about Wyatt. I’ve thought of something.”
“What’s that?”
“A way of getting him out of that bank. If it comes off, that’s to say.”
“By Jove, he’d jump at anything. What’s the idea?”
“Why shouldn’t he get a job of sorts out in the Argentine? There ought to be heaps of sound jobs going there for a chap like Wyatt. He’s a jolly good shot, to start with. I shouldn’t wonder if it wasn’t rather a score to be able to shoot out there. And he can ride, I know.”
“By Jove, I’ll write to father tonight. He must be able to work it, I should think. He never chucked the show altogether, did he?”

===Chinese===

傍晚时分，鲍勃去练习网，撞见板球队长独自站在人群外，脸上那副神情，诉说着大规模的天翻地覆。
“怎么了？”鲍勃问。
“没什么，”伯吉斯说，勉强的、狰狞的平静。“只是，据我所见，星期六咱们要拿一支二队去打里普顿了。顺带问一句，你没碰巧也被开除什么的吧？”
“又怎么了？”
“内维尔-史密斯。星期六禁足。就这样。一天之内，一换、二换两位投手全没了。我猜到明天，其余的人也得走一半，星期六咱们只好拿一队初中部小毛孩子凑数的杂牌军上阵了。”
“内维尔-史密斯！呃，他干什么了？”
“显然他庆祝进一队摆了一桌宵夜，怀亚特就是从那儿回来被摮的。你猜怎么着，内维尔-史密斯今天放学竟自己跑到老头子那儿，把前前后后全招了！说全怪他。胡扯！这种事谁碰上谁倒霏。怀亚特不去他那儿，多半也会熗去别处。”
“老头子就罚他禁足了？”
“接下来两个星期六。”
“里普顿今年强吗？”鲍勃问，想不出更好的话。
“据说强得很。他们灭了马利波恩板球俱乐部。那支马利波恩也相当辣。比咱们打平的那支还强。”
“哦，呃，板球的事说不准。兴许这回轮到我接住一个球了。”
伯吉斯哼了一声。
鲍勃继续往练习网去。迈克正绑护腿。
“我说，迈克，”鲍勃道。“我正找你。是怀亚特的事。我想出个主意。”
“什么主意？”
“把他从那家银行捞出来的法子。要是办得成的话。”
“天哪，他什么条件都肯干。什么法子？”
“他为什么不去阿根廷混个事由？像怀亚特这样的人，那边该有大把正经工作。头一条，他一手好枪法。我毫不怀疑，在那边会打枪也算一桩本事。他还会骑马，这我知道。”
“天哪，我今晚就写信给爸。我想他一定有办法。他没把那边整个撂下吧？”

===Original===

Mike, as most other boys of his age would have been, was profoundly ignorant as to the details by which his father’s money had been, or was being, made. He only knew vaguely that the source of revenue had something to do with the Argentine. His brother Joe had been born in Buenos Aires; and once, three years ago, his father had gone over there for a visit, presumably on business. All these things seemed to show that Mr. Jackson senior was a useful man to have about if you wanted a job in that Eldorado, the Argentine Republic.
As a matter of fact, Mike’s father owned vast tracts of land up country, where countless sheep lived and had their being. He had long retired from active superintendence of his estate. Like Mr. Spenlow, he had a partner, a stout fellow with the work-taint highly developed, who asked nothing better than to be left in charge. So Mr. Jackson had returned to the home of his fathers, glad to be there again. But he still had a decided voice in the ordering of affairs on the ranches, and Mike was going to the fountainhead of things when he wrote to his father that night, putting forward Wyatt’s claims to attention and ability to perform any sort of job with which he might be presented.
The reflection that he had done all that could be done tended to console him for the nonappearance of Wyatt either that night or next morning﻿—a nonappearance which was due to the simple fact that he passed that night in a bed in Mr. Wain’s dressing-room, the door of which that cautious pedagogue, who believed in taking no chances, locked from the outside on retiring to rest.

===Chinese===

同绝大多数同龄男孩一样，父亲的的钱是怎么挣下的、正在怎么挣着，迈克一概茫然不知。他只模糊地知道，进项的来路同阿根廷沾边。哥哥乔生在布宜诺斯艾利斯；三年前，父亲还去那边跑了一趟，想必是办事。凡此种种似乎都表明：想在那黄金国阿根廷共和国找份差事，老杰克逊先生是个用得上的好人脉。
其实，迈克的父亲在内陆拥有大片田产，无数绵羊在那里生生不息。他早已不再亲自督理产业。同斯潘洛先生一样，他有一位合伙人——一位劳动癖病入膏肓的壮实家伙——此人别无所求，只求把大权留给自己。于是杰克逊先生回到了祖先的家园，乐得重归故里。但在 ranch 大牧场的各项事务上，他说话依然算数。当晚迈克写信给父亲，力荐怀亚特值得关照、且无论遇上什么差事都拿得起来——这信等于直奔了源头。
想到能做的都做了，怀亚特当夜和次日早晨始终没露面，也就多少好捱些——没露面的缘故很简单：他在韦恩先生更衣室的一张床上过了那一夜，而那位谨慎的教育家素来信奉一丝风险也不冒，就寝时把他反锁在了门外。

## XXVII The Ripton Match / 二十七 里普顿之战
===Original===

Mike got an answer from his father on the morning of the Ripton match. A letter from Wyatt also lay on his plate when he came down to breakfast.
Mr. Jackson’s letter was short, but to the point. He said he would go and see Wyatt early in the next week. He added that being expelled from a public school was not the only qualification for success as a sheep-farmer, but that, if Mike’s friend added to this a general intelligence and amiability, and a skill for picking off cats with an air-pistol and bull’s-eyes with a Lee-Enfield, there was no reason why something should not be done for him. In any case he would buy him a lunch, so that Wyatt would extract at least some profit from his visit. He said that he hoped something could be managed. It was a pity that a boy accustomed to shoot cats should be condemned for the rest of his life to shoot nothing more exciting than his cuffs.
Wyatt’s letter was longer. It might have been published under the title “My First Day in a Bank, by a Beginner.” His advent had apparently caused little sensation. He had first had a brief conversation with the manager, which had run as follows:
“Mr. Wyatt?”
“Yes, sir.”
“H’m﻿ ﻿… Sportsman?”
“Yes, sir.”
“Cricketer?”
“Yes, sir.”
“Play football?”
“Yes, sir.”
“H’m﻿ ﻿… Racquets?”
“Yes, sir.”
“Everything?”
“Yes, sir.”
“H’m﻿ ﻿… Well, you won’t get any more of it now.”
After which a Mr. Blenkinsop had led him up to a vast ledger, in which he was to inscribe the addresses of all outgoing letters. These letters he would then stamp, and subsequently take in bundles to the post office. Once a week he would be required to buy stamps. “If I were one of those Napoleons of Finance,” wrote Wyatt, “I should cook the accounts, I suppose, and embezzle stamps to an incredible amount. But it doesn’t seem in my line. I’m afraid I wasn’t cut out for a business career. Still, I have stamped this letter at the expense of the office, and entered it up under the heading ‘Sundries,’ which is a sort of start. Look out for an article in the *Wrykynian*, ‘Hints for Young Criminals, by J. Wyatt, champion catch-as-catch-can stamp-stealer of the British Isles.’ So long. I suppose you are playing against Ripton, now that the world of commerce has found that it can’t get on without me. Mind you make a century, and then perhaps Burgess’ll give you your first after all.

===Chinese===

里普顿比赛那天早晨，迈克收到父亲的回信。下楼吃早饭时，盘子上还躺着一封怀亚特的信。
杰克逊先生的信短，但都在点上。他说下星期头几天就去看怀亚特。他又说，被公学开除并不是当牧羊农场主成功的唯一资质，不过，倘若迈克的朋友在此之外还有一份起码的聪明与和善，还有一手气枪打猫、李-恩菲尔德打靶心的本事，那就没有理由不能给他安排点什么。无论如何他会请他吃顿午饭，这样怀亚特这趟至少能榨出些利润。他说他希望能有办法。一个惯于打猫的少年，竟被判处在余生中射击的最刺激之物不超过自己的袖口，未免可惜。
怀亚特的信长些。简直可以拿去发表，题为《一个新手记：我在银行的第一天》。他的降临显然没有引起什么轰动。他先同经理有过一段简短谈话，内容如下：
“怀亚特先生？”
“是，先生。”
“唔……搞体育的？”
“是，先生。”
“打板球？”
“是，先生。”
“踢足球？”
“是，先生。”
“唔……墙网球？”
“是，先生。”
“样样都来？”
“是，先生。”
“唔……那好啊，往后一样也轮不着你了。”
此后一位布伦金索普先生领他到一本巨大的账簿前，他的任务是把所有发函的地址誉上去。誊完再给信件盖章，随后成捆送往邮局。每星期还得买一回邮票。“我要是那些金融拿破仑中的一个，”怀亚特写道，“想必该做假账，再挪用邮票到骇人听闻的数目。可这不像我的路子。恐怕我天生不是经商的料。不过，这封信我已用公费贴了票，并记在‘杂项’科目下，算是某种开端。留意《雷金人》上的文章：《少年罪犯须知，作者：杰·怀亚特，不列颠群岛抓啥偷啥派偷票冠军》。回见。想必你正打里普顿那场——既然商业界发现没有我就转不动。务必打个百分，那样伯吉斯说不定到底会把一队发给你。三年前就发过十二顶队色，因为有家伙期中走了，顶替他的人对里普顿大放异彩。”

===Original===

There were twelve colours given three years ago, because one chap left at half-term and the man who played instead of him came off against Ripton.”
---
This had occurred to Mike independently. The Ripton match was a special event, and the man who performed any outstanding feat against that school was treated as a sort of Horatius. Honours were heaped upon him. If he could only make a century! or even fifty. Even twenty, if it got the school out of a tight place. He was as nervous on the Saturday morning as he had been on the morning of the M.C.C. match. It was Victory or Westminster Abbey now. To do only averagely well, to be among the ruck, would be as useless as not playing at all, as far as his chance of his first was concerned.
It was evident to those who woke early on the Saturday morning that this Ripton match was not likely to end in a draw. During the Friday rain had fallen almost incessantly in a steady drizzle. It had stopped late at night; and at six in the morning there was every prospect of another hot day. There was that feeling in the air which shows that the sun is trying to get through the clouds. The sky was a dull grey at breakfast time, except where a flush of deeper colour gave a hint of the sun. It was a day on which to win the toss, and go in first. At eleven-thirty, when the match was timed to begin, the wicket would be too wet to be difficult. Runs would come easily till the sun came out and began to dry the ground. When that happened there would be trouble for the side that was batting.
Burgess, inspecting the wicket with Mr. Spence during the quarter to eleven interval, was not slow to recognise this fact.
“I should win the toss today, if I were you, Burgess,” said Mr. Spence.
“Just what I was thinking, sir.”
“That wicket’s going to get nasty after lunch, if the sun comes out. A regular Rhodes wicket it’s going to be.”
“I wish we *had* Rhodes,” said Burgess. “Or even Wyatt. It would just suit him, this.”
Mr. Spence, as a member of the staff, was not going to be drawn into discussing Wyatt and his premature departure, so he diverted the conversation on to the subject of the general aspect of the school’s attack.
“Who will go on first with you, Burgess?”
“Who do you think, sir? Ellerby? It might be his wicket.”
Ellerby bowled medium inclining to slow.

---
——这个念头迈克自己也早想到了。里普顿之战是特别赛事，凡对该校立下任何出众战功者，都被当作一位霍拉提乌斯对待。荣誉往他头上堆。他若能打出一个百分！哪怕五十分也行。哪怕二十分，只要能救学校出险境。星期六早晨他紧张得和马里波恩比赛那天早上一样。如今是凯旋，否则进威斯敏斯特教堂。就他拿一队的机会而言，只打出平平的分数、混在人堆里，与不上场同样无用。
星期六早晨起得早的人都看得分明，这场里普顿之战多半不会打平。星期五整日细雨几乎下个不停。夜里深了才停；早晨六点，又一个热天万事俱备。空气里有种感觉，表明太阳正努力钻出云层。早饭时天空一片沉灰，只有一处洇出更深的暖色，透着太阳的口风。这是该赢下掷签、先上场击球的日子。十一点半比赛开打时，场地会湿得没了凶险。太阳一出、开始烤地之前，分数唾手可得。而那一来，轮到击球的一方就要遭殃。
十点三刻课间，伯吉斯同斯彭斯先生一起查看场地，这个道理他一看便明。
“我要是你，今天必赢掷签，伯吉斯。”斯彭斯先生说。
“我也是这么想的，先生。”
“太阳一出来，这场地午后就要变刁。要变成一块地道的罗兹场地。”
“我倒希望咱们*有*个罗兹。”伯吉斯说，“哪怕有个怀亚特也行。这场正合他。”
斯彭斯先生身为教职员，可不想被拖进关于怀亚特和他提前离校的讨论，便把话头引到本校投球实力的总体面相上。
“谁跟你先开投，伯吉斯？”
“您看呢，先生？埃勒比？说不定是他的场子。”
埃勒比投中速偏慢。

===Original===

On a pitch that suited him he was apt to turn from leg and get people out caught at the wicket or short slip.
“Certainly, Ellerby. This end, I think. The other’s yours, though I’m afraid you’ll have a poor time bowling fast today. Even with plenty of sawdust I doubt if it will be possible to get a decent foothold till after lunch.”
“I must win the toss,” said Burgess. “It’s a nuisance too, about our batting. Marsh will probably be dead out of form after being in the Infirmary so long. If he’d had a chance of getting a bit of practice yesterday, it might have been all right.”
“That rain will have a lot to answer for if we lose. On a dry, hard wicket I’m certain we should beat them four times out of six. I was talking to a man who played against them for the Nomads. He said that on a true wicket there was not a great deal of sting in their bowling, but that they’ve got a slow leg-break man who might be dangerous on a day like this. A boy called de Freece. I don’t know of him. He wasn’t in the team last year.”
“I know the chap. He played wing three for them at footer against us this year on their ground. He was crocked when they came here. He’s a pretty useful chap all round, I believe. Plays racquets for them too.”
“Well, my friend said he had one very dangerous ball, of the Bosanquet type. Looks as if it were going away, and comes in instead.”
“I don’t think a lot of that,” said Burgess ruefully. “One consolation is, though, that that sort of ball is easier to watch on a slow wicket. I must tell the fellows to look out for it.”
“I should. And, above all, win the toss.”

Burgess and Maclaine, the Ripton captain, were old acquaintances. They had been at the same private school, and they had played against one another at football and cricket for two years now.
“We’ll go in first, Mac,” said Burgess, as they met on the pavilion steps after they had changed.
“It’s awfully good of you to suggest it,” said Maclaine, “but I think we’ll toss. It’s a hobby of mine. You call.”
“Heads.”
“Tails it is. I ought to have warned you that you hadn’t a chance. I’ve lost the toss five times running, so I was bound to win today.”
“You’ll put us in, I suppose?”
“Yes﻿—after us.”
“Oh, well, we shan’t have long to wait for our knock, that’s a comfort. Buck up and send someone in, and let’s get at you.”
